534

535                           SECTION III.

536 RESOURCING, DEPLOYMENT, AND EXECUTION GUIDANCE

537

538 With the OPLAN approved, staff officers then convert the plan into an effective OPORD. Critical

539 staff tasks involve follow-up and supervision to ensure mission support resources are deployed

540 and synchronized to successfully execute the NBC tasks identified in the OPLAN. Periodic

541 reassessments of the JIPB, facts, assumptions, and “details” such as unit/resource availability,

542 provide necessary updates for improving the NBC defense annex(es) to the OPORD. This

543 section focuses on NBC defense resource considerations; deployment planning, including task

544 organization techniques; and employing NBC defense assets for specific mission execution.

545

546                           FIXED SITE NBC DEFENSE RESOURCES

547

548 Operational planners track and maintain asset availability and visibility during planning,

549 deployment, and execution. Resourcing begins during course of action development. As staffs

550 develop COA, they recommend the best combination of resources to support the mission and

551 COA. As the situation develops and planners identify required functions, recommendations for

552 the time-phased force deployment list (TPFDL) emerge. These recommendations should heed

553 basic resource considerations and should not be restricted by current task organization. Basic

554 resource considerations include Mission Requirements, Resource Capabilities, and Resource

555 Availability/Visibility.

556

557 Mission Requirements. Specified, implied, and essential tasks require force allocation and

558 resource planning for task execution. NBC defense tasks require that all forces maintain

559 individual protection and self decon capabilities, as well as operational decon capability.

560

561 Resource Capabilities. As planners develop force allocation and resourcing plans, they assess

562 force and equipment capabilities from a broad, practical, expedient viewpoint versus a narrow

563 definition of “published” capability. For example, 125 gpm pumps found in numerous fuel support

564 units are not defined as decon support equipment; however, adding a hose with fire fighting

565 nozzle provides an operational decon capability. This operational approach expands the

566 commander’s employment options and adds flexibility during mission execution.

567

568 Resource Availability/Visibility. Planning and execution of fixed site defense measures

569 involves all base/base cluster activities. Failure to provide, or delays in providing support

570 adversely impact base activities. Total asset visibility is critical, and this task is made especially

571 difficult due to the many transient tenants found on or near fixed sites. Command emphasis, staff

572 planning/supervision/follow-up, and asset visibility can help eliminate most resource availability

573 issues such as:

574 • Deployment Priorities

575 • Mission Priorities

576 • Time/Space Factors

577 • Maintenance Status

578 • Inventory Status

579 • Resupply Rates, and

580 • Requisition Status.

581

582 Understanding force and resource capabilities, including those outside the current task

583 organization or DOD, helps operational planners provide flexibility of NBC defense support with

584 available resources.

585

586 Resource Types and Sources. Primary sources of NBC defense assets include DOD units and

587 NBC defense equipment. Functionally, NBC defense equipment can be classified IAW the NBC

588 defense principles: avoidance, protection, and restoration. Additionally, fixed site NBC defense

     3-12


---

                                  Fixed Site NBC Defense Planning and Execution

589 resource needs may require international/coalition or HN support. Further, CCD requirements

590 could necessitate use of smoke and obscurants. Appendix B provides detailed resourcing

591 information and options for use during planning. It is designed to address capabilities useful for

592 fixed site NBC defense and does not address all the specialized variations of some NBC defense

593 equipment, for example: aircraft variations of protective masks.

594

595 Deployment planning is key to ensure the proper mix and type of NBC defense resources. Task

596 organizing and deploying required capabilities and resources synchronized with execution

597 requirements depend on successful deployment planning.

598

599                               DEPLOYMENT PLANNING

600

601 Operational planners use two fundamental products for deployment planning: the Commander’s

602 Task Organization and the TPFDL. Effective command and staff communication with regard to

603 these products is mandatory. Otherwise, entire units and unit sets of equipment could sit idle at

604 an air or sea port of embarkation; combat or combat support resources not available to support

605 the mission. Once resources enter the movement/logistics pipeline, the deployment system

606 provides total asset visibility, even in transit. The challenge: quickly move tremendous tonnage

607 and volumes of resources through a strategic pipeline with finite throughput capacity. How is it

608 accomplished? Mission Focused Task Organization and TPFDL.

609

610 Task Organization. The commander task organizes the force to accomplish mission

611 requirements; however, mission task organization and timelines are impacted by available air and

612 sealift deployment capabilities. The keys to force design (task organization) are flexibility,

613 balance, and mission focus. The joint task force is organized to achieve maximum effectiveness

614 from each contributing component (i.e., USAF, USN, USMC, USA). Commanders determine the

615 proper mix of force capabilities in order to maintain flexible, effective, and efficient support to the

616 mission. Task organization decisions are always mission-focused - every deployed asset must be

617 able to support the mission.

618

619 For example, the existence of a biological threat may not support the deployment of an entire
620 Biodetection company. Mission analysis may establish a requirement for only the C2 element and

621 one platoon. Unless the mission is a “demonstration” of biological defense capability, the

622 deployment of the entire unit would waste valuable strategic lift capability. Table 3-4 provides

623 ideas for building modular Army NBC defense deployment packages. These packages are

624 designed to (1) support mission-specific NBC defense tasks; and (2) provide flexible support to

625 other “non-standard” chemical unit tasks. The concept entails providing economy of force while

626 supporting mission execution and command flexibility. Similar methodology applies to other

627 Service NBC defense capabilities as well.

628

629 Time-Phased Force Deployment List (TPFDL). Once the commander determines task

630 organization and deployment priorities, the deployment sequence and timelines for the task-

631 organized units are established. The commander and supporting movement control centers use

632 time-phased force deployment data (TPFDD) to:

633 • Maximize Transport Throughput Capacity Based on Command Established Mission Priorities

634 • Resolve Transportation Conflicts Based on Mission Priorities and

635 • Synchronize Force Arrival with Mission Priorities/Time Requirements.

636

637 NOTE: TPFDL priorities will change based on JIPB updates and/or changes. NBC staff officers

638 will closely monitor the deployment of NBC defense resources, to include verifying their status on

639 the TPFDL. Resources not appropriately prioritized to mission requirements could arrive too late

640 to support the mission. Resources not prioritized or even listed would wait for movement

641 indefinitely.

642

644

645                         FIXED SITE NBC DEFENSE EXECUTION

646
647 The fixed site environment requires clear, concise mission guidance for effective NBC defense
648 execution. However, a clear, concise mission order does not guarantee successful mission
649 execution. Success is never assured; however, certain measures can support critical preparation
650 for the successful conduct of NBC defense. These measures include:

651

652 • Anticipate Enemy COA. Wargame the NBC defense plan periodically. Continue to “What if”

653  the probable COA as new intelligence develops, and modify friendly mission requirements

654  appropriately. Just like friendly forces, the enemy desires the least predictable COA that

655  supports his objectives.

656

     3-14


---

                           Fixed Site NBC Defense Planning and Execution

657 • Critique the Plan. After individual plan critique, ask a staff officer of a different specialty or

658  Service to critique the NBC defense plan. If he were the enemy, what weaknesses would he

659  target? If he were a subordinate commander or tenant unit, could he execute the plan with no

660  additional guidance?

661

662 • Check, Check, and Re-Check Resource Status. Asset visibility is a critical component of

663  NBC defense. Locating resources following a WMD attack creates a reactive environment

664  supporting enemy intent. Implement measures to ensure all base personnel, including

665  civilians, have personal NBC protection or have a nearby consolidated issue point for quick

666  distribution. Do not hesitate to seek command assistance with stalemated issues.

667

668 • Rehearse, Rehearse, Rehearse. At home station, forces train to perform individual and

669  collective NBC defense tasks as units and Joint forces. However, once alerted or mobilized

670  for deployment, all training is a rehearsal for executing combat missions. Units will move in

671  and out of the fixed site, sometimes daily, creating new, unknown variables that may hinder

672  successful execution. HN civilians supporting base operations need frequent rehearsals and

673  retraining.

674

675 • Track the Battle. Appendixes D and E provide tools for implementing the NBC defense plan

676  and for synchronizing NBC defense actions based on identified threat “triggers” or events.

677  However, unless the NBC defense staff tracks the friendly and enemy situation, these tools

678  become ineffective for maintaining a proactive NBC defense.

679

680 • Plan and Execute Redeployment as Thoroughly as Deployment Operations. NBC

681  defense and environmental aspects of redeployment, retrograde of personnel and equipment,

682  and post-conflict remediation are extensive and are addressed separately in Appendix I.

683

684 • Mission Focus. Frequently ask, “Does this support the Mission?”; “Will this support the

685  Commander’s Intent and Criteria for Success?”. Stay alert to intelligence indicators reflecting

686  possible changes in enemy objectives and anticipate their mission implications. If the

687  Commander, Chief of Staff, Plans Officer, or Operations Officer is deciding COA, be there to

688  provide NBC defense recommendations or to determine current NBC defense plan

689  implications.

690

691 Regardless of how well the plan has addressed enemy courses of action, our forces must

692 respond to NBC hazards quickly, precisely, and as a team. Referring to Operation DESERT

693 STORM, Joint Pub 1, Joint Warfare of the US Armed Forces, says, “...the most striking feature of

694 this campaign was the high degree of teamwork...for the cohesion and efficiency in the

695 components were blended into a higher order of trust and confidence in the joint team....” This

696 citation goes beyond command and support relationships established in plans/orders and hinges

697 on trust, cohesion, and common objectives.

1

2                                 OVERVIEW

3
4 This appendix provides a common start point for identifying requirements to conduct a successful
5 fixed site NBC defense. Unique situations will generate unique mission requirements that
6 preclude a “text book” solution. However, the thought process in the discussion and examples,
7 combined with experience using the referenced documents, should provide staff focus during
8 operational planning.

 9
10 The Universal Joint Task List, Version 3.0, CJCSM 3500.04A (UJTL) provides an interoperability
11 tool for Joint Force Commanders to communicate mission requirements in terms of joint tasks,
12 conditions, and standards. It also serves as a common language and reference system for JFC,
13 operational planners, combat developers, and trainers. When synchronized with Service specific
14 universal task lists (UTL), the UJTL becomes a tool for ensuring interoperability and integration
15 down to the tactical level of task execution. These UTL allow commanders to develop their
16 mission essential task list (METL) and help focus training and mission requirements.

17

18 This appendix is provided for the same reason  as a tool to assist commanders at various levels
19 with the development of their mission supporting NBC METL. Although many of these tasks do
20 not directly apply to the Base Cluster/Base Commander, they do provide insight into specified and
21 implied tasks at higher levels of command.

22

23 NOTE: A quick review of these tasks reveals the apparent omission of intelligence,

24 surveillance, and reconnaissance tasks. This appendix was written assuming that an NBC

25 threat capability already exists and that it was identified as a battlefield condition in the

26 basic OPLAN. It also assumes that intelligence collection efforts continue to define the

27 NBC threat environment.

28

46

47 Baseline tasks at the operational level directly influence the fixed site base cluster/base

48 commander as well as units operating in and around fixed sites, ports, and airfields. The

49 supporting NBC defense tasks shown adjacent to the baseline tasks (Table A-3) may be specified

51 associate required tasks to specific mission resource requirements equating to capabilities and

52 functions. As the operational concept develops, the task organization evolves to provide those

53 required capabilities/functions at the right time and location (Time Phased Force Deployment and

54 Synchronization).

     Appendix A

75                                SCENARIO EXAMPLES

76

77 The following examples demonstrate the application of the UJTL task analysis process to derive

78 specified and implied NBC defense tasks. These tasks become mission requirements that must

79 be resourced in subsequent plans/orders and supplemented with thorough staff supervision and

80 follow-up.

81

82 Although not demonstrated, the same fundamental approach carries through the Service UTL as

83 well.

84

85                                Persian Gulf Illustration

86

87 In the early hours of August 2, 1990, an Iraqi force of more than 100,000 personnel, spearheaded

88 by three armor divisions, invaded Kuwait.

89

90 On August 7, the U.S. National Command Authority directed the deployment of US forces in

91 response to Saudi Arabia’s request for assistance. USCENTCOM responded rapidly, placing the

92 first US forces on the ground within 31 hours of the initial alert order.

93

94 Operation DESERT SHIELD was a two-phase operation. The first phase involved the initial

95 deployment of forces to deter further Iraqi aggression and to defend Saudi Arabia. The second

96 phase included the subsequent deployment of forces to resource the coalition with a robust

97 counteroffensive capability that could evict the Iraqi Army from Kuwait. Although some units did

98 not begin to deploy until late November, they were in place and combat-ready by early February

99 1991.

100

101 The tailoring of a proper force mix for this operation required the mobilization of thousands of

102 guardsmen and reservists-the largest mobilization since Word War II. During this force-projection

103 operation, the services deployed nearly a half-million personnel, their supporting equipment, and

104 60 days of supplies from the United States and Europe. All this was pipelined through fixed sites,

105 ports, and airfields within a period of six months.

106

107 During this operation, the existing WMD threat influenced commanders’ selection of mission

108 essential tasks. The focus at each level of command was mission accomplishment and

109 protecting the force; however, the specific tactics, techniques, and procedures used at the various

110 echelons differed.

111

112                               Persian Gulf Example 1

113

114 Using the Persian Gulf illustration, Table A-8, Example 1 illustrates how application and use of the

115 NBC warning and reporting system (NBCWRS) varied from the Strategic to the Tactical Level.

116

117 • NBCWRS use at the Tactical Level (e.g., fixed sites) emphasized reacting and reporting key

118  information.

119 • Operational Level focus emphasized attack confirmation and/or issuance of Selective or

120  General TMD warnings.

121 • Strategic Theater considerations emphasized establishment of:

122       • Interoperable theater warning network

123       • Theater level assessments and

124       • Forwarding of applicable national level intelligence.

125 • Strategic National concerns involved:

126       • Providing forward deployed forces with requisite resources (e.g., personnel,

127            equipment, units)

128       • Forwarding WMD related intelligence information

129       • Assessment of evaluated report information and

130       • Political-military concerns with regard to targeting and/or potential retaliatory strikes.

     A-4

134                               Persian Gulf Example 2

135

136 Using the same Persian Gulf illustration, Table A-8, Example 2 outlines possible fixed site force

137 structure considerations at various echelons of command.

138

139 • Tactical Level collocated army, air force, or HN assets rehearse combined/joint thorough

140  decon operations.

141 • Operational Level commanders and staff:

142  • Estimate decontamination requirements for water, supplies, equipment, and unit

143           needs

144  • Conduct chemical unit task organization and

145  • Establish applicable command or support relationships.

146 • Strategic Theater commanders and staff prioritize the flow of NBC defense units and other

147  resources by determining who, when, and where (e.g., TPFDL considerations) various assets

148  will arrive.

149 • Strategic National considerations involve factors such as furnishing required assets and

150  providing strategic mobility for deployment.

     Appendix A

152                Stability Operations and Support Operations Illustration 1

153 On April 20, 1999, National Command Authority announced the beginning of a Show of Force on

154 request from the government of Cortinia. By April 21, 1999, US aircraft had begun airlift of US

155 forces to the AO. In turn, US military personnel moved to pre-positioned combat vehicles and

156 deployed to training areas for combined live fire training exercises. Concurrently, USMC units,

157 including armor elements, began offloading and preparing for rehearsals with Cortinian armored

158 forces. The armor unit basic load contained DU munitions. Further, extensive amounts of

159 particulate matter in the atmosphere restricted line-of-sight visibility due to “slash and burn”

160 deforestation and neighboring country volcanic eruptions.

161

162                Stability Operations and Support Operations - Example 1

163

164 Table A-9, Example 1 indicates the identified tasks and corresponding actions at each command

165 level.

166

167 • JCS concerns specified NBC protection as a METL task and furnished applicable technical

168  and safety information and subject matter experts (SME) from other federal agencies.

169 • The CINC (Strategic Theater) ensured inclusion of NBC protection in the METL, and used

170  available SME to conduct initial risk and hazard assessments.

171 • The JTF Commander conducted detailed NBC hazard assessments, and provided hazardous

172  materials (HAZMAT) training guidance to deploying units.

173 • The USA and USMC deploying personnel received HAZMAT briefings and training, to include

174  DU awareness training.

178

179               Support Operations Illustration 2

180

181 On February 1, 1999, National Command Authority tasked USCENTCOM to conduct a site

182 assessment prior to deployment of a JTF to Krasnovia for Nation Assistance Operations. The first

183 elements of the Site Assessment Team (SAT) arrived in Krasnovia February 4, 1999.

184 USCENTCOM mission intent specified that force protection and troop safety was crucial. Initial

185 investigation indicated key facilities and terrain vicinity a proposed JTF fixed site were possibly

186 contaminated by toxic industrial chemicals during prolonged occupation by a neighboring military

187 power. The HN lacked the capability to determine extent and level of contamination.

188 Subsequently, requests were submitted to augment the SAT with additional resources including

189 USAF Civil Engineers and USA Medical and NBC resources. These personnel and equipment

190 including NBC Reconnaissance Systems arrived February 15, 1999 and began their risk

191 assessments.

192

193  Stability Operations and Support Operations - Example 2

194

195 Table A-10. Example 2 indicates the identified tasks and corresponding actions at each
196 command level.

197

198 • Strategic National considerations emphasized:

199  • Force Protection

200  • Overall Assessments, and

201  • Crisis Action Teams providing guidance and decisions on emerging issues.

202 • Strategic Theater efforts furnished:

203  • Required Resources, and

204  • IPB, Risk Assessment, and Vulnerability Analysis Products.

205 • The JTF Commander provided:

206  • A SAT Command and Control Element

207  • Conducted Task Organization

208  • Coordinated HN Logistics and Security Support, and

209  • Provided IPB and Risk Assessments from SAT Input.

210 • The Augmented SAT Conducted:

211  • Standard Assessments

212  • NBC and Toxic Industrial Chemical Reconnaissance and

213  • Determined/Reported Extent of Contamination and Provided NBC and Medical

214  Analysis and Evaluations.

2                                                OVERVIEW

3

4 The purpose of the following series of tables is to provide the Fixed Site Commander with a planning tool to

5 identify Service, unit organization, and/or international resources that may be available to contribute to the

6 NBC defense of the base cluster/base. Understand that this appendix does not provide an all inclusive

7 NBC defense listing, but intends to cue planners to additional sources of support to augment organic

8 capabilities. As resource cues, international data referenced from Jane’s NBC Protection Equipment (97-

9 98), and has not been verified by the USACMLS or other government agencies. Specific items of NBC

10 defense equipment, as well as the corresponding units’ capabilities, are addressed where applicable.
11

    Appendix B

20                                              ARMY

21
22 All Army units are capable of conducting limited NBC Defense operations. These operations consist of, but
23 are not limited to, detection of nuclear and chemical contamination; performing basic decontamination
24 procedures on individuals and equipment; deployment of nuclear and chemical detection devices; and
25 conduct of nuclear and chemical monitoring, survey, and reconnaissance mission operations.

26
27 The Army Chemical Corps force structure includes specialized units providing additional capabilities for
28 Nuclear, Biological, and Chemical (NBC) detection, identification, survey, reconnaissance, and thorough
29 decontamination. Chemical Corps units also provide large area smoke and obscurant support to
30 operations.

31

32                             Chemical Company (Heavy Division)

33
34 The Chemical Company (Heavy Division) is capable of providing the following support:

35          • Four Detailed Equipment Decontamination (DED) sites (52 major items/day/site); Operational

36          Decontamination; Special Decontamination

37          • Large area smoke (0.2 to 1.1 km wide x several km long, weather dependent); Simultaneous

38          coverage of several small areas when employing smoke generator squads on separate

39          missions

40          • Radiation monitoring for the nuclear accident and incident control plan (NAICP)

41          • Chemical detection/monitoring for chemical accident and incident control plan (CAICP)

42          • NBC Recon (route, area, and zone); surveys, surveillance, and sampling

43          • NBC staff to division headquarters; operates on 24-hour basis.

44

45  The Chemical Company (Heavy Division) is comprised of:

46          • Division chemical section         • Decontamination platoons (4)

47          • NBC center                        • Mechanized smoke platoon (1)

48          • Headquarters section              • NBC reconnaissance platoon (1)

49

214                                               AIR FORCE

215
216 NBC defense in the Air Force is focused on the three key areas of avoidance, protection, and
217 decontamination. All members receive training in basic NBC defense procedures. This training provided
218 by Civil Engineer Readiness Flight personnel consists of classes on Attack Actions, Warning and
219 Reporting, Individual Protective Equipment, Collective Protection, Personal Decontamination, and
220 Contamination Control Procedures. Additionally, Air Wings designate personnel within their units to serve
221 on special teams that support installation NBC defense. These teams, known as Shelter Management
222 Teams (SMT), Contamination Control Teams (CCT), and Disaster Preparedness Support Teams (DPST)
223 provide the base with support in the areas of NBC decontamination, detection, and survey operations. The
224 following table depicts items of NBC defense equipment unique to the Air Force.

225

                                                     facepiece and two voicemitter assemblies.

     Collective Protection Equipment                                     Description

     Survivable Collective Protection System II      Modular concrete collective protective shelter with
                                                     integral overpressure and filtration systems..

     KMU-450F Building Modification Kit              Overpressure/filtration system for pre-existing
                                                     structures/buildings.

               Detection Equipment                                       Description

     AN/PSR-2 Automatic Liquid Agent Detector        Portable point detector for fixed installations, (e.g.,
                                                     airfields). Detects liquid concentrations of GD, VX,
                                                     HD, and L.

     M90 Chemical Warfare Agent Detector             All-purpose chemical agent vapor detector. Detects
                                                     vapor concentrations of blood, blister, and nerve
                                                     agents. Can also be used to monitor contaminated
                                                     surfaces using intake tubes.

     Automatic Mustard Agent Detector                Information TBP

     ADM 300 Multifunctional Radiation Detection     Auto-ranging RADIAC instrument designed to detect
     Instrument w/Probes                             alpha, beta, and gamma radiation.

227                                  Readiness Flights

228

229 The Civil Engineer Readiness Flight is the organization that is responsible for all actions and measures
230 taken to protect air base resources from the effects of attack, natural disasters, and major accidents, as
231 well as restoring these resources following disasters. Additionally, they provide specialized training to SMT,
232 CCT, and DPST members. Readiness Flight is a component of the Prime BEEF Team.

233

256                                           NAVY

257
258 The Navy does not operate using specialized NBC defense units. Personnel required to function in this
259 area are drawn from existing job specialties. Naval units will generally posses only enough NBC equipment
260 and consumables for their own support. The Navy addresses NBC defense in two primary areas: Units
261 Afloat and Units Ashore. Shipboard units have repair parties and teams capable of conducting limited NBC
262 detection, decontamination, and contamination control operations on-board their assigned vessel. Shore-
263 based units have disaster preparedness teams capable of providing more comprehensive assistance in the
264 form of organized and equipped teams. In addition to these disaster preparedness teams, Naval
265 Construction Forces and their associated engineer equipment may provide assistance with contamination
266 control based on mission priorities. The following table depicts items of NBC defense equipment unique to
267 the Navy.

276                                MARINE CORPS

277
278 With the exception of the Chemical Biological Incident Response Force (CBIRF), the Marine Corps does
279 not use structured NBC defense units. USMC capabilities in this appendix are based on unit equipment
280 and individual/collective training. Marines receive training in NBC detection, protection, and
281 decontamination operations. USMC NBC defense personnel include warrant officers (MOS 5702) and
282 NBC Specialists (MOS 5711) who are responsible for manning NBC control centers, training units, and
283 maintaining NBC equipment. Personnel intensive tasks such as unit decontamination and NBC
284 reconnaissance operations are performed as an additional duty by Marines from within the unit. The
285 following table depicts specialized items of NBC defense equipment within the Marine Corps.

288  Chemical/Biological Incident Response Force (CBIRF)

289
290 The Chemical/Biological Incident Response Force (CBIRF) is the only structured NBC defense unit in the
291 Marine Corps. Comprised of approximately 350 Marines and sailors, the unit is capable of providing NBC
292 reconnaissance, personnel/casualty decontamination, medical support and expert advice through the

                                                                                                       B-15


---

     Appendix B

293 Electronic Reachback Advisory Group (ERAG). The ERAG is an organization chartered to assist with the
294 development, training, and operations of the CBIRF. It is composed of eight nationally and internationally
295 recognized civilian experts in science and medicine. The ERAG is self-contained, self-sufficient, and
296 rapidly deployable worldwide. The following table depicts the specialized/unique NBC defense equipment
297 organic to this unit.

298

           Decontamination Equipment                Description

     Specialized Shower System                 TBP

           Detection Equipment                      Description

     Alarm Chemical Agent, Remote Sensing M21  Standoff nerve and blister agent detector. Range is
                                               line of sight to 5 km.

     Reconnaissance System, NBC M93A1 FOX      Vehicle mounted system designed to detect,
                                               identify, and mark NBC contamination.

           Miscellaneous Equipment                  Description

     Reverse Osmosis Water Purification Units  Water purification system capable of treating water
     (ROWPU)                                   from any available source. Will remove NBC
                                               contaminants. Powered by a 30 kW generator.
                                               Production Rate: Seawater source: 600 GPH.
                                               Fresh water source: 1800 GPH.

299

300                             Marine Expeditionary Force (MEF)

301
302 The MEF is the Marine Corps principal warfighting organization. It consists of a combination of air, ground,
303 and support assets. These units can range in size from less than one infantry division to multiple infantry
304 divisions and aircraft wings. NBC control centers are formed at all levels of command down to
305 battalion/squadron level, and are manned by trained Marine Corps personnel. The MEF is capable of
306 conducting NBC detection, reconnaissance, and decontamination operations.

313

318                              General Overview

319

320 The fixed site commander must demonstrate the ability to use all available resources to support potential
321 contingencies. Apart from DOD, the armed forces of most potential allied/coalition partners currently
322 possess significant quantities of specialized decontamination, detection, and protection equipment as well
323 as various types of decontaminants. The following tables have been extracted from Jane’s NBC
324 Protection Equipment, 1997 – 98. Equipment capability has not been further validated by the USACMLS
325 or CBDCOM, Aberdeen Proving Ground, MD. The tables provide a categorized list of equipment by
326 selected country.

354

355                                           COVERS

356

357 The use of buildings and covers to protect supplies and equipment can provide significant benefits

358 to the fixed site commander. Limiting the exposure of these items to NBC agents reduces the hazard,
359 and subsequently the need for decontamination. As a general rule anything that provides a barrier
360 between the items of concern and the environment will provide some degree of protection. Currently there
361 are NBC protective covers (NBC-PC) available as Common Table of Allowance (CTA) items. These
362 covers are specifically designed to provide a 24-hour barrier from liquid agent contamination.

363
364 Covering can also be accomplished with items as basic as canvas tarpaulins and plastic sheeting, or as
365 elaborate as Large Area Maintenance and Tactical Aircraft Shelters. The following table depicts some
366 examples of material and equipment that may be available to the fixed site commander for this purpose.
367 These items generally provide protection from liquid agent hazards, however those with integral
368 overpressure/filtration systems additionally provide vapor hazard protection. In all cases a significant
369 degree of protection will be afforded to the personnel, equipment or material, either covered or contained
370 within.

371

372 Note: Caution should be exercised when using commercially available protective covers/material in

373 place of military issued covers. These items may not possess the multispectral camouflage

374 qualities of their military counterpart and would subsequently be subject to detection by enemy

375 acquisition systems.

391

392 In addition to the resources available through military supply channels, assets may be available from the

393 host nation. Use of these facilities will not only reduce turn around time, but will also alleviate some of the

394 burden placed on military logistics channels. Examples of these include, but are not limited to:

395        • Local Police Departments

398        • Local Retail Centers

400        • Local Fire Departments

402

404 evacuation of the public from toxic areas. (e.g., French Fire Brigades)

                                 APPENDIX C

    VULNERABILITY ANALYSIS AND MITIGATION

1

2                                       OVERVIEW

3 Vulnerability analysis is the continuous, systematic estimating of friendly consequences from

4 threat NBC attacks. The overall methodology includes IPB, risk assessment, and vulnerability

5 analysis with associated mitigation measures. This appendix provides a “how to“ approach for

6 risk assessment and a discussion on vulnerability analysis that allows the commander to

7 determine his/her units vulnerability and how to reduce that vulnerability.

8

9                            RISK ASSESSMENT

10

11 The charts (figures C-1 to C-3) on the following pages describe the risk assessment process

12 designed to aid the commander and staff in determining force risk levels and the minimum

13 recommended steps to reduce the NBC risk. Follow these basic steps when using the risk

14 assessment charts:

15  • Enter the chart at “Start Here”.

16  • Answer the main question in the shaded box by considering subordinate

17  questions/answers underneath it.

18  • If the answer to any question below the shaded box is a “yes,” then the shaded box

19  answer is “yes.”

20  • Go to the next lower box and repeat the process.

21  • If the answer to a shaded box question is “no,” read the risk assessment to the right.

22  • Read to the right to determine the minimum recommended procedures to reduce risk.

23  • Complete the assessment by writing the assessed risk level in the risk assessment box at

24  the bottom of the page.

25

26 Commanders and staffs should modify these charts as they identify additional questions and

27 mitigation measures based on mission specific situations.

---

                                                     Vulnerability Analysis and Mitigation

32

33                                NUCLEAR VULNERABILITY

34

35 To assess a unit's vulnerability to nuclear attack, the commander determines the unit's NBC

36 defense protection level and the type and size of weapon likely to be employed by the enemy.

37 The commander then weighs various courses of action (COA) to determine which COA allows for

38 mission accomplishment at an acceptable risk.

39

40 When addressing unit vulnerability to nuclear weapons employment, consider; blast or shock

41 wave, thermal radiation or heat, initial or prompt radiation, and residual radiation (fallout), and

42 EMP effects. The potential exists for an enemy to employ a weapon which produces only one of

43 these effects; e.g., radioactive dust particles, EMP. Therefore, assess vulnerability to each effect,

44 not just the greatest effect.

45

46 A nuclear explosion's biological effects are measured according to the amount of radiation

47 (centigrays) to which personnel are exposed. For the biological effects of radiation in man refer to

48 Table A-2, FM 3-3-1/FMFM 11-18, Nuclear Contamination Avoidance or appropriate Service

49 publication.

50

51 Two techniques to evaluate unit vulnerability to nuclear detonations are:

52  • A technical approach in which unit dispositions are compared with the effects of an

53         expected yield, and

54  • An operational approach in which unit dispositions are compared with targeting criteria

55         used by the threat target analyst.

56

57 In a nuclear environment, the more concentrated a unit is, the more lucrative a target it becomes.

58 If the unit itself is not the target, but falls within the fallout pattern, unit monitors will be capable of

59 providing the commander with essential information regarding the hazard. Nuclear hazard

60 prediction is addressed in FM 3-3-1/FMFM 11-18, Nuclear Contamination Avoidance or

61 appropriate Service publication.

62

63 The primary tool for analyzing friendly dispositions is the radius of vulnerability (RV). RV is the

64 radius of a circle within which friendly troops will be exposed to a risk equal to, or greater than, the

65 emergency risk criterion (5 percent combat ineffectiveness) and/or within which material will be

66 subjected to a 5 percent probability of the specified degree of damage (see the RV tables in JP 3-

67 12.2 (SRD), Nuclear Weapons Employment and Effects Data, or JP 3-12.3, Nuclear Weapons

68 Employment and Effects Data (Notional) (unclassified for training purposes). The ground zero

69 (GZ) for the RV is always assumed to be the point where detonation will do the greatest damage

70 to the unit or installation. Delivery errors are not considered. For RV of unspecified categories,

71 see comparable table chart in JP 3-12.2 or JP 3-12.3.

72

73 Based on vulnerability radii and unit size, commanders may determine risk from a nuclear attack

74 and whether or not to adjust unit dispersion. However, personnel may not be the targets. Often

75 equipment, due to sensitivity and vulnerability, becomes the target. See FM 3-3-1/FMFM 11-18,

76 Nuclear Contamination Avoidance for training data. For actual vulnerability radii refer to JP 3-12.2

77 (SRD).

78

79 Analyzing the vulnerability of friendly dispositions and installations consists of:

80         • Determining, the appropriate threat yields based on current intelligence. Determining

81               the disposition of unit personnel.

82         • Obtaining the appropriate vulnerability radii from the RV table (JP 3-12.2 (SRD) or JP

83               3-12.3 or FM 3-3-1 for training purposes).

84         • Estimating fractional coverage for each target category, using the visual, numerical,

85               or index technique. For information concerning these techniques reference JP 3-12.2

86               (SRD) (visual technique is discussed in FM 3-3-1/FMFM 11-18 for training purposes).

                                                                                        C-5


---

     Appendix C

87           • Recommending ways to decrease vulnerability and increase protection.

88

89 To determine vulnerability using the visual technique, outline the unit battle position. Use a

90 compass, a piece of plastic with the RV drawn to scale on it, or a circular map scale.

91 Superimpose the RV chosen from Table C-1 or JP 3-12.2 over the predicted targeted area.

92

93 The GZ used for the analysis is the location that would result in the highest fractional coverage of

94 the target. From this worst case GZ and the appropriate RV, an estimation of the percentage of

95 casualties or materiel damage that might result from an enemy nuclear strike may be determined.

96

97 Using the center point of the compass, template, or circular map scale as the GZ, choose the GZ

98 that would result in the highest fractional coverage of the target area. Visually estimate the

99 percentage of the unit covered by the RV.

100

101 If this fractional coverage yields unacceptable losses of personnel or equipment, the commander

102 must then make a decision of how best to reduce the casualty and equipment loss rate. This may

103 be done by adding shielding or enacting vulnerability reduction measures. If a mechanized

104 battalion occupies a battle position 5 km wide and 2.5 km deep, it could be positioned as in Figure

105 C-4. Target elements are uniformly dispersed in the area. In this example, the RV from a 5-

106 kiloton weapon for personnel in armored personnel carriers is 1250 meters (as determined from

107 Table C-1). Worst case the RV by placing the GZ where it provides the largest target coverage.

108 Fifty percent of the battalion is covered by the RV, thus up to 50 percent of the battalion’s

109 personnel in armored personnel carriers could become casualties. When the same battalion

110 deploys in three company battle positions in depth, the distances between positions significantly

111 reduce the damage probability, even assuming the weapon detonates at the worst case GZ. As

112 seen in Figure C-5, although one company is 100 percent vulnerable, the battalion overall is only

113 33 percent vulnerable.

114

131  BIOLOGICAL VULNERABILITY

132

133 Prior to conducting vulnerability analysis, determine the risk of a biological agent attack or the

134 enemy's capability and probability of use (see page C-3). Once it is determined that the enemy

135 has the capability and the willingness to employ biological weapons; the next step is to determine

136 the unit's vulnerability to an attack (table C-2).

137

138 Note: Remember, even if an enemy has no capability to employ biological weapons, the unit is

139 still vulnerable to endemic diseases.

140

141 To determine vulnerability to biological agents:

142  • Determine immunization levels in relationship to threat/theater endemic agents and

143  availability of prophylaxis.

144  • Determine unit's protective posture.

145  • Determine unit’s biological detection posture - do you have BIDS, Interim Biological Agent

146  Detection System (IBADS), LRBSDS, or Joint Point Biological Detection System (JPBDS).

147  • Determine unit's hygienic practices. For example, are troops provided means to

148  bathe/cleanse regularly?

149  • Determine current or projected maneuver (or mobility) disposition.

150

151 Vulnerability ratings are subjective. Apply rating measures in relationship to probable agent of

152 choice. Also, ratings do not consider troop motivation/morale factors. The final rating provides a

153 general vulnerability analysis and should be used as a basis for a thought-process leading to

154 sound recommendations on vulnerability reduction measures.

            8 - 9                              LOW                          Minimum actions resulting from
                                                                           these ratings are described below.
            10 - 16                            MEDIUM

            17 - 24                            HIGH

     LOW:   ••Maintain current efforts. Attempt to improve on those areas that are weak.

     MEDIUM: ••Analyze current actions and increase efforts to reduce rating - concentrate on those areas that you
                         have immediate control over (e.g., MOPP levels & hygiene and possibly detection assets).

     HIGH:  ••Analyze current actions and immediately increase efforts to reduce rating - concentrate on those
            areas that you have immediate control over (e.g., MOPP levels and hygiene).
            ••If you don’t have operational control of detection assets, determine where these assets are and if you

               are inside the detection “umbrella” or if these assets can be repositioned to cover your operation.
            ••Determine if immunization rates are satisfactory for the total force. Typically contract workers, from

               whatever source will require more immunizations than US military personnel. Provide
               immunizations as soon as medical and political situations allow. Remember that immunizations
               require time to work effectively.

     * If “mask only” protective posture provides required protection for predicted agent, use a value of 2.

156

157                  EXAMPLE BIOLOGICAL VULNERABILITY ANALYSIS

158

159 1. Begin at the left column and add the relative values from each column.

160

161         • Approximately 30% of unit has been immunized.

162         • Unit is currently in MOPP ZERO.

163         • Unit has a BIDS attached.

164         • Unit practices good hygienic measures.

165         • Unit is in a static fixed site.                                                       NOTE:
                                                                                     Read the vulnerability
166                                                                                  rating at the bottom of the
                                                                                     chart to determine minimum
167 2. Adding the values shows a -             4 for Immunizations                   steps to take to reduce
                                                                                     rating.
168                                            6 for MOPP level

169                                            4 for Detection posture

170                                            1 for Hygiene

171                                            3 for Disposition

172                                      TOTAL = 18 = High Vulnerability

173
174 This analysis provides a basis to advise on vulnerability (and the reasoning used), and more
175 importantly, provide vulnerability reduction measures at the same time.

176

     C-8


---

                Vulnerability Analysis and Mitigation

177             CHEMICAL VULNERABILITY

178

179 Prior to conducting a vulnerability analysis, first determine the risk of a chemical attack or the

180 threat’s capability and probability of use (see figure C-3).

181

182 If the possibility exists for the threat to employ chemical agents, conduct a vulnerability analysis in

183 two parts:

184  • First, make an estimate of the threat's capability to employ chemical munitions in the

185         unit's AO/Al (see page C-4, Chemical Risk Assessment) within a specific time period.

186  • Second, use this information to generate simplified effects information.

187

188             ESTIMATE DELIVERY CAPABILITY

189

190 Step 1. Determine time periods of interest. Time periods of interest are determined based on

191 the commander's operational concept and situation variables, such as mission, enemy, terrain,

192 troops, time, and civilian consideration or METT-TC. The time period is determined in

193 coordination with the intel and operations officers. They will normally conform to phases or the

194 expected duration of an operation; however, it may be desirable to use other criteria.

195

196 For example: A maintenance unit may want to use the expected time lag between an anticipated

197 threat chemical attack and the time required to retrieve and don their protective gear (as in

198 "MOPP READY" protective posture) as the time period of interest. A time period may also be

199 based on factors relating to enemy tactics, such as the expected arrival time of a second echelon

200 force. Further, significant weather changes could also influence the selection of time periods.

201 The time period of interest can range from six- hours to 48-hours.

202

203 Vulnerability analysis is generally conducted in support of the planning process, not in support of

204 current operations. While some planning factors are based on a 12-hour to 48-hour cycle. Fixed

205 site operations may be based on a significantly higher time frame (i.e., 12-hours to 96 hours), with

206 time periods of 24-hours or greater used when IPB allows. Time periods of less than six- hours

207 are generally not used. For short-term actions, shorter time periods could be used to estimate the

208 effects of initial enemy preparation fires or to estimate the effect of a single chemical agent attack.

209

210 Step 2. Associate weather data with each time period.

211

212 Associate each time period with a temperature, wind speed, and stability category. The

213 temperature will impact primarily on agent persistency. For each time period, temperature should
214 be expressed as one of the following (in degrees Celsius): 55o, 50o, 40o, 30o, 20o, 10o, 0o, -10o, -
215 20o, or -30o.

216

217 NOTE: All required information can be obtained from the Chemical Downwind Message

218 (CDM).

219

220 Determine temperature by taking the average of the temperatures from each CDM line applicable

221 to the time period of interest. Use this average temperature for all calculations.

222

223 NOTE: When estimating persistency for agents expected to last beyond the time period of

224 interest, use the average daily temperature of the day in which the attack may occur.

225

226 Wind speed will impact on casualty production, persistency, and downwind agent travel. It should

227 be expressed as one of the following: 3, 6, 9, 12, 15, or 18 kmph.

228

229 NOTE: As a rule of thumb, for any wind speed above 18 kmph, use 18 kmph. Calculate

230 wind speed in the same manner used above for temperature. In some situations it may be

231 necessary to modify this number for casualty estimate purposes.

                                                                                                  C-9


---

     Appendix C

232

233 For example: If a 24-hour period contains six-hours of expected high wind speeds (unstable

234 conditions), you will probably elect to disregard those figures and develop a separate (lower)

235 average for casualty estimation. The staff estimates an enemy would not employ chemicals for

236 casualty effects during that six-hour period of high winds. Base this decision on the magnitude

237 and duration of the wind change and the expected enemy COA.

238

239 Stability categories (stable, neutral, or unstable) also affect casualty production and downwind

240 agent travel. However, their impact is minor compared to temperature and wind speed.

241 Determine the stability category in the same way as temperature and wind speed.

242

243 Other environmental factors exist that could impact the analysis.

244

245 For example: Terrain and vegetation could affect the estimate. However, these factors have

246 been incorporated in the persistency estimate process.

247

248 Step 3. Estimate delivery capability.

249

250 Estimate the number of chemical munitions likely to be employed in your AO for each required

251 time period. Coordinate with the intelligence officer and/or fire support officer (FSO), if available,

252 to produce this estimate.

253

254  • Provide the intel officer with the time periods of interest.

255  • The intel officer can produce information concerning the threat's capability to deliver

256        chemical munitions in your AO.

257

258 The estimate should indicate the number of delivery units, by type, and the number of rounds, by

259 agent, if available. The intel officer also provides estimates on when, where, and what type of

260 agent the enemy will use in the AO. If the situation or event template does not yield needed

261 information, assume the enemy can optimize the agent mix.

262

263 For example: To determine the threat's capability to create a contamination obstacle, assume

264 they will fire only persistent agents. Likewise, to predict casualty effects, assume the enemy will

265 fire agents that have the greatest casualty producing effects.

266

267 When the primary threat is covert or unconventional, express enemy delivery capability in terms of

268 agent weight or as agent weight times some expected delivery means.

269

270 For example: Ten kilograms of nerve agent delivered by an agricultural sprayer. If estimates

271 indicate limited agent supply, it will be difficult to estimate how much of that supply will be used

272 each day. As an option for this situation, conduct the analysis for a single enemy attack based on

273 the threat's maximum employment capability during the selected time period.

274

275 The intelligence officer considers a number of factors in making his estimate:

276        • Number of employment assets within range of the AO

277        • Other AO the enemy force must service. (NOTE: Do not assume every delivery

278        system within range will be firing into the AO being considered.)

279        • Enemy locations of chemical munitions

280        • Weather effects on probable agents

281        • Threat forces' capability to deliver chemical munitions to delivery systems

282        • Impact of threat attacks on civilians

283

284 The intelligence estimate should provide a range of numbers based on estimated COA for each

285 time period. The estimate should provide the enemy's maximum capability and his likely delivery

286 capability. Alternatively, different estimates can be provided that would support various enemy

     C-10


---

                                                      Vulnerability Analysis and Mitigation

287 COA. Estimates should not be based on friendly COA unless they would significantly impact on

288 enemy delivery capability.

289

290 It is not necessary to assess every possible situation and enemy option. To do so would result in

291 inefficient use of available time. The goal is to provide estimates to the commander/staff, which

292 can be later refined. Continuously assess the situation and look for events and options with the

293 potential of changing the outcome of the battle.

294

295                    GENERATE EFFECTS INFORMATION

296

297 At this point you have estimated time periods and munitions estimate combinations. For each of

298 these combinations, develop a set of effects information: casualty estimates, contamination

299 obstacles, persistence, and times and locations of downwind agent effects. Effect information will

300 provide the following estimates:

301  • Casualty effects.

302  • Downwind agent effects.

303

304                                Determine Casualty Estimate

305

306 Step 1. Determine probable friendly targeted size.

307

308  • Based on the chemical staff’s and S2/G2 IPB, select an area/activity the enemy would

309            probably target then determine the target size. For example, determine the area

310            occupied by a fixed site activity, in this case 400m x 600m.

311

312 Calculate the number of hectares (ha) in the selected target area. For Example: One hectare is

313 10,000 square meters; therefore, an area that is 400m x 600m = 240,000 square meters or 24

314 hectares.

315

316 Step 2. Determine probable agent. Unless it is known which agents the threat will employ,

317 assume the most effective casualty-producing agent available.

318

319 Step 3. Estimate casualties.

320

321 Estimate casualties based on:

322  • IPB

323  • The number of rounds the threat may use to engage the specific target

324  • Predicted temperatures (from CDM or other sources)

325  • Then refer to tables C-4, C-5, and C-6 for corresponding casualty percentages.

326

327 Note: For Tables C-4, C-5, and C-6, if the number of rounds falls between given numbers

328 assume worst case by rounding up to the next higher number.

329

330 Casualty estimates. The casualty estimates are valid for wind speeds less than 20 kmph. Other

331 factors such as air stability category, humidity, variations in wind speeds under 20 kmph, and

332 delivery errors were found to have minimal effect on casualty estimates for a given time period as

333 opposed to a specific point in time.

334

335 For example: The templated target area is 24 hectares, predicted agent is GB, the temperature is
336 10o C (degrees Celsius), and the weapon is 152mm gun/howitzer. Intelligence analysis estimates

337 the enemy will fire 240 rounds at the target. 240 rounds divided by 24 hectares is 10 rounds per

338 hectare. Go to Table C-4 and extract approximate casualty percentage (50%). To determine

339 blister agent casualties, use the same procedures and table C-6; however, use MOPP level rather

340 than temperature.

                                                                                       C-11

346

347                            Determine Downwind Hazard Risks

348

349 Associated risks from downwind hazards (see ATP-45 (A)/FM 3-3/FMFM 11-17 or appropriate

350 Service publication for downwind prediction models) can be broken into three categories:
351
352 High casualty risk. Occurs at winds speeds of 10 kmph or less during slightly stable, stable, or

353 extremely stable atmospheric conditions. Agent clouds will produce very narrow and very long

354 hazard clouds. Dosages of 100 times the lethal levels are possible in the hazard area.
355
356 High degradation risk. Occurs during stability categories of neutral to very unstable and wind

357 speeds less than 10 kmph. Agent clouds will produce wide hazard areas with lethal effects rarely

358 extending as far as 10 kilometers. The casualty risk to warned, unmasked personnel is low.

359 However, due to the large cloud width it is possible for every unit in the downwind hazard area to

360 be forced to mask for several hours.
361
362 Low casualty risk. Occurs at wind speeds of 10 kmph or greater at stability categories of neutral

363 to very unstable. The casualty risk is very low outside the area of immediate effects. Although a

364 significant number of units will be forced to mask, agent duration will be short and will not extend

365 as far as in previous categories.

     C-12


---

                                 APPENDIX D

    FIXED SITE NBC DEFENSE PLANNING TOOLS

1

2                                     OVERVIEW

3

4 This appendix supports the discussion in Chapter 3 by further focusing NBC defense

5 considerations on fixed site operations. The format used is the standard OPORD format with a

6 brief statement as to the type information which might be addressed under that portion of the site

7 OPORD. Because of the multiple tenant activities normally found on a base or within a base

8 cluster, it is important that all tenants operate in concert and respond to NBC situations in a like

9 manner. Organizing the entire base NBC defense under one plan will help preclude some tenants

10 being protected and others “not getting the word.”

11

12 The following sections, Mission Template (Section I); NBC Annex (Section II); NBC

13 Recon/Surveillance/Monitoring Appendix (Section III); and Smoke Support Appendix (Section IV)

14 are designed to stimulate thought as to the type information to be included. The sections are not

15 intended to exclude the application of specific mission considerations not addressed in this text.

16

17                   KEY POINTS FOR ISSUING ORDERS

18

19 This section provides several general rules for preparing and issuing good orders.

20

21                                    PREPARATION

22

23 • Clarity. Clearly convey your NBC defense message through NBC doctrine, use of approved

24  acronyms and abbreviations, and use of established military terminology.

25 • Completeness. Include all the information and instructions necessary to coordinate and

26  execute NBC defense operations. If more information is forthcoming, state expected

27  time/content. Do not leave open ends.

28 • Brevity. Avoid providing unnecessary NBC defense details; however, do not sacrifice clear

29  and complete orders for brevity.

30 • Use the affirmative. Direct expressions serve to convey simple, clear meanings to

31  instructions.

32 • Avoid qualifying directives. Meaningless expressions and adverbs do not clearly fix

33  responsibility for actions.

34 • Authoritative expressions. Be direct, decisive, and clear in fixing mission needs, support,

35  and responsibility.

36 • Timelines. Do your planning early and continuously; permit maximum incorporation of NBC

37  defense into all phases of planning and implementation.

38

39                                    ISSUING ORDERS

40

41 • Give simple and direct NBC defense missions.

42 • Give the units time to plan and prepare for mission execution.

43 • Use the one-third/two-third rule; backwards plan to allow subordinates two-thirds of the total

44  time available.

45 • Obtain a backbrief to ensure comprehension.

46

47                                    SECTION I.

48                               MISSION TEMPLATES

49

50 Mission templates provide a “road map” for planning, preparing, and successfully executing

51 operations. The NBC defense staff uses doctrinal templates (see figures D-1 to D-5) to plan

52 NBC defense operations, to employ NBC reconnaissance, biological detection, smoke and

                                                                                       D-1


---

     Appendix D

53 decontamination assets, and to conduct battle management. This section provides tools for

54 developing OPORDS and employing chemical assets.

55 Each template provides the process for planning, preparing, and executing the chemical support

56 mission from the staff’s perspective. The boxes on the template serve as memory aids and

57 some tasks are self-explanatory.

58

59 The staff templates chemical missions that support these operating systems:

60   • Reconnaissance, Surveillance and Intelligence: NBC reconnaissance and biological

61        detection support CCIR collection efforts.

62   • Mobility and Survivability: Avoidance, protection, and decontamination measures directly

63        support mobility and survivability efforts along with friendly smoke/CCD operations.

64   • Command and Control: Tracking and controlling the disposition of NBC defense assets

65        provides options for flexible NBC defense execution.

66 • Combat Service Support (CSS): Providing force protection, sustained C2, and sustained

67        combat support requires attention to details of mission resourcing. Limited resources equal

68        limited flexibility.

69

70 The following pages provide base cluster/base commanders and staffs guidance and mission

71 templates for NBC defense operations, NBC reconnaissance, biological detection, smoke, and

72 decontamination operations.

73

74               NBC DEFENSE OPERATIONS TEMPLATE

75

76 The NBC Defense Operations template (figure D-1) is used to evaluate the enemy’s ability to

77 employ WMD against fixed site operations and to delineate the assets required to mitigate WMD

78 effects. This template is used to summarize what is known and not known about the enemy’s

79 NBC capabilities and to determine information required. With this information, the staff can

80 perform an NBC Vulnerability Assessment evaluating the impact of NBC attack on base mission

81 accomplishment; site operations (some operations will be more vulnerable to degradation than

82 others); and individual mission performance factors. Finally the template provides a quick

83 reference to the status of units attacked or in the downwind hazard area of an NBC attack. Units

84 are only posted to this last status matrix when they are at risk. When no longer at risk, they may

85 be deleted from the matrix.

86

87                              NBC RECONNAISSANCE TEMPLATE

88

89 The NBC Reconnaissance Template (figure D-2) is used to summarize the reconnaissance

90 missions and organizations charged with hazard detection and monitoring on a base, site, or base

91 area of interest. The mission box lists missions assigned by the base commander for conducting

92 NBC reconnaissance. These assignments can be NBCRS unit missions; missions tasked by

93 higher headquarters to BIDS and LRBSDS teams operating within the base area of operations; or

94 it can be survey and monitoring missions assigned to sector commanders or base tenant

95 activities. More specific base/base cluster NBC reconnaissance missions may include medical

96 treatment personnel collecting specimens from suspect NBC patients for analysis, preventive

97 medicine surveillance of drinking water sources and supplies; and veterinary services surveillance
98 of potential food supplies and government owned animals. The C2 box highlights the Command

99 and Control relationships between the NBC reconnaissance, surveillance, and survey teams, their

100 locations, and the NAI they will operate in or against. The Communications box provides contact

101 information and the Sustainment box lists critical items required to perform reconnaissance

102 operations. The site commander and staff should study the NBC Defense Operations and the

103 NBC Reconnaissance templates to ensure that areas potentially targeted by the enemy are

104 covered in the base Reconnaissance plan.

105

     D-2


---

     Fixed Site NBC Defense Planning Tools

106  BIODETECTION TEMPLATE

107

108 The Biodetection Template (figure D-3) is very similar to the Reconnaissance template except that

109 the Mission block refers to the Corps Commander’s mission for biodetection operations rather

110 than the base commander’s intent and mission phases. Additionally, the NAI and UTM grid

111 coordinates are generally assigned by the Corps and coordinated with the base commander.

112 Unique sustainment items such as BW detector tickets and BIDS/LRBSDS components are

113 provided through the Biological Detection Company. Common supplies and maintenance may be

114 tasked to the base logistics support operations.

115

116  SMOKE TEMPLATE

117

118 The Smoke template (figure D-4) helps organize smoke missions assigned to smoke delivery

119 elements including projected, emplaced, and generated obscurants. Combat vehicle self-

120 protection smokes are not included. The Sensor Window box helps the smoke planner determine

121 the precise types of obscurants required to counter enemy sensors. The planner then determines

122 the best distribution of available smoke assets for accomplishing the base’s obscuration priorities.

123 Unlike maneuver operations, base smoke missions will likely rely heavily on landline
124 communications for mission C2. Therefore, both radio frequencies and phone numbers of

125 supported and controlling elements should be recorded.

126

127  DECONTAMINATION TEMPLATE

128

129 The Decontamination template (figure D-5) helps assess decontamination requirements based

130 upon friendly IPB (i.e., how many targets is the enemy capable of attacking simultaneously).

131 Agent type and anticipated quantities drive how much decontamination might be required and is
132 quantified as “required throughput”. The C2 box lists priorities for decontamination based upon

133 the site commanders’ assessment of critical activities requiring the most immediate return to

134 normal operations. Additionally, this box specifies decontamination site locations, link-up points,

135 security requirements, and COA affecting decontamination operations. Under the Sustainment

136 window, special attention is required for site closure procedures, as the decontamination site,

137 once used, will remain a hazardous materials site requiring remediation as time and base

138 priorities permit. This template entry should reflect the tentative plan for closure and remediation

139 and confirm notification of the responsible engineer activity.
